Table 6
Developing Influence Through Teaching, Growth Challenges, and Role Modeling
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 6, “Develop Influence”?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains influence as a capability that can be developed through deliberate, ethical behavior rather than a fixed personality trait or a position of authority. Its central model has three mutually reinforcing practices: teach people how to think, challenge them to grow, and role model the way. The aim is to help people think better about themselves, others, and the world; raise their standards for character, relationships, and contribution; and demonstrate the values and behaviors being encouraged. Influence is therefore measured less by control over people and more by the positive effect a person has on their choices, growth, collaboration, and results. The chapter uses leadership and personal stories to show how these practices can change relationships and organizational performance.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the three practices of the Ultimate Influence Model and identify one relationship in which each could be applied."
Key point:
- Influence is developed through teaching, constructive challenge, and example.
- Ethical influence helps others grow and contribute rather than simply comply.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
- Penguin Random House: High Performance Habits
2. Q: How does the chapter define influence?
A: Show answer
The chapter defines influence broadly as the ability to shape other people’s beliefs and behaviors so that they are more likely to believe in an idea, follow a direction, make a purchase, or take an action being requested. This definition focuses on observable effects, not merely on the influencer’s intention or self-image. It also emphasizes that influence is a two-way process: the person seeking to influence others should remain open to learning from them and being influenced in return. In practical terms, influence involves understanding what matters to another person, communicating in a way that helps them think, inviting meaningful action, and behaving consistently with the values being promoted. It does not guarantee agreement, and it does not remove another person’s right to decide.
Study instruction:
- "Write the definition in your own words and distinguish the intention to influence from evidence that another person has actually been influenced."
Key point:
- Influence concerns the effect on beliefs and behavior, not status or charisma alone.
- People retain agency; influence is not a guarantee of agreement.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
- Penguin Random House: High Performance Habits
3. Q: How is influence different from authority, popularity, or coercion?
A: Show answer
Authority comes from a formal role or legitimate decision right; it may allow a manager to assign work, approve expenditure, or set a deadline. Popularity is being liked or socially accepted. Coercion seeks action through threats, pressure, or fear. Influence, as developed in the chapter, relies on relationships, ideas, constructive challenge, trust, and example so that people understand and willingly support a direction. A person may have authority without earning trust, or popularity without being able to mobilize a team around a difficult objective. Strong ethical influence can complement formal authority, but it cannot replace lawful instructions, clear accountability, or required controls. It should expand understanding and commitment rather than suppress legitimate disagreement.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a time when someone complied because of authority with a time when someone acted because they understood and supported the purpose."
Key point:
- Authority can require compliance; influence seeks informed and willing engagement.
- The strongest leadership uses role authority responsibly while building trust and shared commitment.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
4. Q: Can someone develop influence if they are introverted or do not consider themselves a natural people person?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter rejects the assumption that influence belongs only to extroverted, charismatic, or naturally persuasive people. It presents influence as a collection of behaviors that can be practiced: making clear requests, asking thoughtful questions, listening, offering useful help, appreciating people, setting constructive challenges, and modeling the conduct expected of others. A reserved person may build influence through preparation, reliability, expertise, careful listening, and consistent follow-through. The practical task is not to imitate an outgoing personality but to identify which behaviors are effective and authentic for the individual. Social confidence can develop through repeated practice, feedback, and reflection; personality should not become an excuse for avoiding important conversations.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two influence behaviors that suit your communication style and practice them deliberately during the next week."
Key point:
- Influence is a learnable set of behaviors, not an extroversion test.
- Authentic listening, preparation, and follow-through can be influential.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
5. Q: Why does the chapter describe asking as a fundamental influence skill?
A: Show answer
People often fail to ask for help, support, feedback, resources, or commitment because they anticipate rejection or fear being judged. The chapter argues that these predictions can be inaccurate and that people frequently underestimate others’ willingness to respond positively. A clear request makes an opportunity for cooperation visible; without asking, the other person may not know what is needed or how they could help. Asking is also a way to test assumptions rather than silently treating them as facts. A good request explains the purpose, specifies the action, gives relevant context, and leaves room for an honest answer. Asking does not create entitlement: the other person may decline, negotiate, or request more information.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one constructive request you have postponed and write a concise version that gives the other person a genuine choice."
Key point:
- A respectful request creates an opportunity for cooperation that silence cannot create.
- The response must be accepted without retaliation or pressure.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
6. Q: How can someone overcome the fear of being judged or rejected when making a request?
A: Show answer
First, separate the possibility of rejection from the assumption that rejection is certain or humiliating. Define exactly what you are asking for and why it matters; then consider what a reasonable person would need to decide. Make the request clearly and at an appropriate time instead of hinting, apologizing excessively, or trying to make the other person guess. If the answer is no, ask whether the reason is timing, capacity, priorities, or a genuine disagreement, and decide whether a different proposal is appropriate. Do not turn a refusal into evidence that the person dislikes you. The chapter’s practical message is to act rather than allow imagined judgments to make the decision for you, while remaining courteous and accepting boundaries.
Study instruction:
- "Practice making one low-risk request and record what actually happened rather than what you feared would happen."
Key point:
- Anticipated rejection is not the same as an actual refusal.
- Confidence improves when requests are clear and responses are treated as information.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
7. Q: Why does the chapter recommend asking more than once, and how can repetition remain respectful?
A: Show answer
A useful idea or request may not be noticed, understood, or prioritized the first time. Repetition can increase familiarity and give people another opportunity to engage, especially when the message is relevant and the timing changes. However, repeating a request is not permission to badger someone, disregard a refusal, or apply escalating pressure. A responsible follow-up adds value: it clarifies the outcome, supplies missing information, offers a practical alternative, or asks whether the priority has changed. The influencer should distinguish a request that was missed from a request that was considered and declined. Repetition is constructive when it improves understanding and preserves choice; it becomes coercive when it attempts to wear down resistance.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a follow-up message that adds useful context and explicitly allows the recipient to decline or suggest another option."
Key point:
- Thoughtful follow-up can keep an important idea visible.
- Respect for a clear refusal is part of ethical influence.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
8. Q: How do questions help a leader gain influence more effectively than simply issuing demands?
A: Show answer
Questions reveal what people think, feel, need, value, and hope to accomplish. They also make room for information the leader does not possess. When people contribute ideas to a plan, they can see how their judgment has shaped the result and are more likely to understand the reasoning behind the work. A leader can ask what success would look like, what risks are being overlooked, what support is needed, and what approach the team would recommend. Questions should be sincere rather than disguised instructions with only one acceptable answer. After asking, the leader must listen, reflect the answer accurately, and explain what will be done with the input. This creates participation without pretending that every decision can be made by consensus.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare five open questions for an upcoming meeting and identify how each answer could affect the decision."
Key point:
- Questions uncover perspectives and create participation.
- Asking is meaningful only when the answers are genuinely considered.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
9. Q: What does the principle “people support what they create” mean in practical leadership?
A: Show answer
The principle means that people are more likely to engage with an idea when they have helped shape it. Involving staff in defining a problem, identifying options, setting milestones, and choosing implementation methods can create ownership and reveal practical knowledge that senior leaders might miss. Participation does not mean that every suggestion must be adopted or that decision accountability disappears. The leader should explain the decision rights, constraints, and non-negotiable requirements, then show how contributions were considered. A practical example is asking a project team to design the workflow within agreed budget, safety, and compliance limits. The resulting plan can combine leadership direction with the expertise of those who will carry it out.
Study instruction:
- "Redesign one directive as a bounded consultation in which the team can influence the method but understands the fixed requirements."
Key point:
- Participation increases ownership when people can see how their input matters.
- Leaders remain accountable for decisions and constraints.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
10. Q: How does giving to others strengthen influence without creating an expectation of repayment?
A: Show answer
Giving can build trust and make relationships more reciprocal when it responds to a real need. Useful contributions include sharing relevant knowledge, making a helpful introduction, removing an obstacle, offering feedback, recognizing work, or giving someone the autonomy to act. The chapter contrasts a helpfulness mindset with a transactional approach in which every favor is recorded as a debt. Ethical giving is offered because it creates value, not because the giver intends to demand repayment later. A person should also check whether the help is wanted; unsolicited assistance can undermine another person’s agency or create dependency. Giving is most constructive when it supports another person’s capability and does not conceal a demand.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a useful contribution you can make without attaching an unspoken expectation of return."
Key point:
- Helpful action can strengthen trust and cooperation.
- Giving should respect the recipient’s needs, agency, and capacity.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
11. Q: How should a person avoid giving so much that generosity becomes exhausting or unsustainable?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that perceived giving burnout is often connected to poor energy management and lack of clarity, not simply to the act of helping. That does not mean unlimited giving is healthy. Sustainable generosity requires choosing contributions that matter, managing workload, protecting recovery time, and being honest about capacity. A person can offer a smaller but reliable form of help, refer the request to someone better placed to assist, or agree on a realistic time. Leaders should not use the language of generosity to excuse chronic understaffing, unpaid work, or the erosion of boundaries. The practical balance is to remain helpful while maintaining the energy and focus needed to fulfill existing responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Review one recurring commitment and decide whether to continue, reduce, delegate, or decline it based on its value and your capacity."
Key point:
- Sustainable generosity requires clarity and energy management.
- Boundaries protect the ability to contribute reliably over time.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
12. Q: Why does appreciation matter to influence in a workplace?
A: Show answer
People are more willing to contribute when their effort is noticed and treated fairly. The chapter recommends specific gratitude, public recognition when appropriate, and attention to the contributions that might otherwise be overlooked. The American Psychological Association’s 2016 Work and Well-Being Survey reported that 53 percent of employed adults felt valued by their employer, while 46 percent said they regularly participated in decision-making, problem-solving, and goal-setting activities. These are historical U.S. survey findings, not current universal rates, but they illustrate how recognition and involvement can be weak points in workplace experience. Appreciation should be truthful, timely, specific, and proportionate; generic praise or praise used as a tactic can reduce credibility.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one recent contribution and write a specific acknowledgment that explains its effect on the team or service."
Key point:
- Recognition can help people see that their contribution matters.
- The APA figures describe a 2016 U.S. survey and should not be presented as current global statistics.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
- American Psychological Association: Workplace well-being survey
13. Q: What does it mean to become a champion of the people you seek to influence?
A: Show answer
Being a champion means actively supporting people’s growth, good ideas, and meaningful contributions. It includes learning what matters to them, noticing their strengths, encouraging sound initiatives, giving credit, and creating opportunities for them to take responsibility. It also means addressing barriers that prevent them from succeeding rather than praising them while leaving avoidable obstacles in place. A champion does not approve every idea or shield people from accountability; honest feedback and clear standards remain necessary. The test is whether the person experiences the support as genuine and useful, not whether the leader receives gratitude or loyalty in return.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one person’s strength, one ambition, and one avoidable barrier, then plan a practical way to support progress."
Key point:
- Championing people combines encouragement with practical support.
- Support includes accountability and honest feedback, not unconditional agreement.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
14. Q: How can a leader distinguish genuine impact from simply believing that they are generous?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes feeling that one gives more than peers from feeling that one’s efforts make a meaningful difference. A person may spend substantial time helping but still be solving the wrong problem, creating dependency, or overlooking what the recipient actually needs. Impact should therefore be tested through feedback and observable outcomes: did the support improve someone’s capability, remove a barrier, strengthen a relationship, or help the team deliver? The person receiving assistance should be asked whether the intervention was useful and what would make it better. Self-perception matters, but it should be checked against the experience of others and the consequences of the behavior.
Study instruction:
- "For one recent act of help, ask the recipient what changed and what would have been more useful."
Key point:
- The value of giving is better judged by meaningful impact than by self-image.
- Feedback helps distinguish helpful support from well-intended but ineffective effort.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
15. Q: Why are trust, autonomy, and decision-making authority powerful forms of giving?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes giving people authorship: meaningful choice over what they work on or how they achieve an agreed result. Trust and autonomy communicate that the leader believes the person can exercise judgment. A meta-analysis of leader autonomy support in workplace settings found positive associations with autonomous work motivation, well-being, and positive work behaviors, and a negative association with distress. This supports the value of autonomy-supportive leadership, but it does not mean every task can be left without oversight. Decision rights should match the person’s capability, risk level, role, and applicable controls. A leader can set the desired outcome, boundaries, and review points while allowing the employee to choose the method.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a task where the outcome and constraints are clear, then delegate the method and define proportionate check-ins."
Key point:
- Autonomy can support motivation and constructive work behavior.
- Delegation must remain consistent with competence, risk, and accountability.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
- Leader autonomy support in the workplace: A meta-analytic review
16. Q: What is the Ultimate Influence Model presented in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The Ultimate Influence Model organizes positive influence around three practices. First, teach people how to think about themselves, other people, and the wider world. Second, challenge people to develop their character, connections, and contributions. Third, role model the values and behaviors that the influencer wants others to embody. The model is not a rigid sequence or a script for controlling people. The practices overlap: a leader teaches partly through example, challenges are more credible when the leader models the standard, and role modeling becomes more influential when people understand the purpose. The framework can be used in families, teams, coaching, business, and community relationships.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a three-part version of the model and add one example of each practice from your own work or life."
Key point:
- The model combines teaching, challenge, and role modeling.
- All three practices should serve the other person’s growth and shared purpose.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
- Penguin Random House: High Performance Habits
17. Q: What does “teach people how to think” mean in the Ultimate Influence Model?
A: Show answer
It means deliberately helping people develop a more useful way to interpret themselves, other people, and the world. The chapter is not advocating that leaders dictate what others must believe. It encourages leaders to ask questions, offer frames, explain what they have learned, and help people examine assumptions. A manager might help a team view customer complaints as information about a service gap rather than as personal attacks. A parent might help a child see a difficult assignment as an opportunity to practice persistence rather than proof of inability. Effective teaching invites reflection and gives people tools to form better judgments independently.
Study instruction:
- "Select one unhelpful assumption in a current situation and prepare a question that could help someone examine it."
Key point:
- Teaching influences thinking by expanding perspective and judgment.
- The purpose is to strengthen independent thinking, not demand identical beliefs.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
18. Q: Why does the model ask people to think about themselves, others, and the wider world?
A: Show answer
These three frames shape how a person interprets choices. Thinking about oneself concerns identity, strengths, responsibility, capability, and purpose. Thinking about others concerns empathy, trust, collaboration, and the assumptions made about people’s motives. Thinking about the wider world concerns the environment in which decisions occur: customers, communities, competitors, opportunities, constraints, and future consequences. A leader might encourage an employee to see themselves as a problem-solver, colleagues as partners rather than rivals, and the market as changing in ways that reward learning. The three frames should be connected to evidence and context rather than imposed as slogans.
Study instruction:
- "For one person you want to support, write one constructive question about self, one about others, and one about the wider environment."
Key point:
- Influence can shape identity, relationships, and interpretation of the environment.
- The three frames should increase understanding rather than create blind optimism.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
19. Q: How should someone prepare to teach another person how to think?
A: Show answer
Preparation begins with the desired positive outcome: what action, decision, or capability would be useful? The influencer then considers how the other person might think about themselves, the people involved, and the wider situation. Before the conversation, identify the assumptions that may be driving current behavior and the facts that support or challenge them. Prepare open questions rather than a speech that leaves no room for response. Consider the individual’s background, role, current pressures, and preferred way of receiving information. During the conversation, listen for differences between what the influencer expected and what the person actually believes. Revise the approach when new information warrants it.
Study instruction:
- "Before your next important conversation, write the desired outcome and three questions covering self, others, and the wider context."
Key point:
- Good influence begins with preparation around the other person’s perspective.
- A prepared conversation must still adapt to what the person says.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
20. Q: How can a leader reframe a complaint without dismissing the person’s feelings?
A: Show answer
A complaint can reveal frustration, unmet needs, poor process design, or an opportunity to learn. The leader should first acknowledge the concern and ask what happened, what matters most, and what outcome the person wants. Only then should the leader introduce a different way to interpret the situation. For example, rather than telling an employee that a difficult assignment is exciting, the manager might ask which part feels unclear and help the employee identify a skill they can develop. Reframing is not denying a genuine problem, minimizing harm, or insisting that every difficulty is positive. It is helping the person see more options while treating the original concern seriously.
Study instruction:
- "Take one complaint and rewrite your response so it first acknowledges the problem and then invites a more useful interpretation."
Key point:
- A useful reframe respects the experience before offering another perspective.
- Optimism should not be used to silence valid criticism or evidence of harm.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
21. Q: What do the chapter’s stories about the author’s parents illustrate about teaching how to think?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the author’s parents responding to hardship with resourcefulness, humor, compassion, and a belief that life still offered possibilities. When their home heater failed during a cold winter, they turned a difficult week into an indoor camping experience for their children. The lesson was not that hardship is harmless; it was that people can choose a response that protects connection and hope while dealing with a real constraint. The father’s calm treatment of frustrated customers and the mother’s compassion toward people who treated her poorly also modeled how to interpret others generously. These examples show that beliefs are often taught less by lectures than by repeated behavior in ordinary situations.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a difficult situation in which your response could demonstrate resilience without denying the seriousness of the problem."
Key point:
- People learn ways of thinking from the behavior they repeatedly observe.
- A constructive outlook can coexist with realism about difficult circumstances.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
22. Q: Why does the chapter recommend giving people the benefit of the doubt?
A: Show answer
Assuming that another person may be stressed, uninformed, distracted, or dealing with pressures we cannot see can prevent unnecessary escalation. The chapter’s story of the author’s father illustrates responding to rude customers with patience and practical help instead of assuming that every angry interaction reflects a person’s whole character. Giving the benefit of the doubt creates room to ask what happened and clarify intentions before assigning blame. It does not require ignoring repeated misconduct, accepting unsafe behavior, or abandoning accountability. When evidence shows a pattern of abuse or deliberate harm, the appropriate response may include boundaries, documentation, and escalation.
Study instruction:
- "Practice separating an observable behavior from the story you are telling yourself about the person’s motives."
Key point:
- Generous interpretation can reduce unnecessary conflict.
- Accountability and boundaries still apply when behavior is harmful or repeated.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
23. Q: What does it mean to challenge people to grow in a positive way?
A: Show answer
A positive challenge asks someone to raise their standard, develop a capability, or contribute at a higher level while communicating respect for their potential. It may be subtle, such as asking how their best self would handle a problem, or direct, such as identifying a missed commitment and asking what will change next time. The purpose is growth, not humiliation or a demonstration of the leader’s power. The challenge should be specific enough to guide action and proportionate to the individual’s experience and capacity. A constructive challenge can feel uncomfortable, but the person should understand that the leader is committed to their development and will provide appropriate support.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a vague criticism as a specific challenge that identifies the desired behavior and the support available."
Key point:
- A growth challenge raises expectations while preserving dignity.
- The purpose is development and contribution, not punishment.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
24. Q: Why do intent and tone matter when challenging another person?
A: Show answer
The same words can have very different effects depending on whether they are delivered with respect or contempt. A challenge intended to help someone improve is more likely to be received constructively when the speaker is curious, specific, and willing to listen. A condescending tone, public embarrassment, sarcasm, or personal attack communicates that the goal may be to diminish the person rather than develop them. Leaders should state the behavior or result that needs attention, explain its impact, and ask what change is realistic. They should also be prepared to hear that their own expectations, instructions, or support contributed to the problem.
Study instruction:
- "Practice delivering corrective feedback in a private, factual, and respectful way, then invite the other person’s perspective."
Key point:
- Respectful intent must be visible in the words and delivery.
- A positive label cannot make humiliating behavior constructive.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
25. Q: How can a person challenge someone’s character without attacking their identity?
A: Show answer
The character dimension concerns values and habits such as honesty, integrity, responsibility, self-control, patience, hard work, and persistence. A useful challenge focuses on a choice or behavior and asks the person to consider whether it reflects the values they want to embody. For example, “What would your most responsible response be now?” is more constructive than calling someone irresponsible. The chapter describes questions such as whether a person gave their best effort or what values they were trying to demonstrate. A fair challenge also recognizes situational constraints and avoids treating one mistake as a complete judgment of character.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a behavior that needs improvement and formulate a question that invites reflection on a value rather than attacking identity."
Key point:
- Character challenges connect behavior to values and personal standards.
- Discuss conduct and choices, not a person’s worth or fixed identity.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
26. Q: How can a leader challenge people to improve their connections with others?
A: Show answer
The connection dimension concerns how people communicate, cooperate, resolve conflict, and treat colleagues, family members, or partners. A leader can set explicit expectations for respectful listening, timely feedback, inclusion, support, and constructive disagreement. When someone behaves dismissively or rudely, the leader should address the conduct rather than allow it to become normal. The chapter emphasizes repeated reminders to listen more, respect one another, support each other, and build stronger relationships. These expectations should apply consistently, including to high performers and senior staff. A team’s cohesion is not improved by requiring silence; it is improved when people can disagree without disrespect.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one relationship behavior that would improve your team’s collaboration and define how it can be observed."
Key point:
- Influence includes challenging how people treat and support others.
- Respectful disagreement is compatible with high standards and accountability.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
27. Q: How can a leader challenge people to make a greater contribution?
A: Show answer
Contribution means the value a person creates for others, the organization, or the wider community. The chapter recommends challenges that look beyond correcting today’s output and invite people to improve the future: develop a better service, redesign a process, create a new product, solve an adjacent problem, or share expertise. The challenge should connect to the person’s strengths and role, and should be supported by resources, authority, and realistic priorities. Telling someone simply to “do more” is not enough; the leader must explain what additional value would look like and what trade-offs may be needed. Meaningful contribution is measured by usefulness, not activity volume.
Study instruction:
- "For a current role, define one future-oriented improvement that would create measurable value for a customer, colleague, or community."
Key point:
- A contribution challenge asks how to create more value, not merely more activity.
- The organization must provide realistic authority, time, and resources.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
28. Q: Why should growth challenges be tailored to the individual rather than delivered as one-size-fits-all demands?
A: Show answer
People have different skills, experience, confidence, responsibilities, and constraints. The chapter describes high-performing leaders adjusting the challenge to the person and meeting them where they are. One employee may need a stretch assignment that develops judgment; another may need clearer expectations and practice in collaboration; a third may already be capable of designing a new service. A uniform demand can overwhelm a beginner, under-challenge an experienced specialist, or ignore a disability or workload constraint. Tailoring does not mean lowering standards arbitrarily. It means defining a relevant next step that is ambitious but achievable and matching it with the support needed to succeed.
Study instruction:
- "Select two people with different levels of experience and design a distinct growth challenge for each toward the same shared outcome."
Key point:
- Fair development is individualized while maintaining transparent standards.
- An appropriate challenge matches ambition with capacity and support.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
29. Q: How can a leader challenge high standards without becoming unnecessarily harsh?
A: Show answer
High standards should be expressed as clear expectations, useful feedback, and a commitment to improvement, not intimidation. The chapter argues that many high performers appreciate meaningful challenges, but that observation is not a license to assume every person welcomes every form of pressure. A leader should explain why the standard matters, define the evidence of success, invite the person to identify obstacles, and agree on support and review dates. If performance remains below standard, the next step may be formal performance management, but it should still be fair, documented, and consistent with policy. Challenge becomes harsh when it ignores context, humiliates people, or demands results without resources.
Study instruction:
- "Turn one high-level expectation into a measurable standard, an agreed support plan, and a review date."
Key point:
- High expectations work best when they are clear and supported.
- Challenge should never excuse humiliation or unrealistic workloads.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
30. Q: What does “role model the way” mean in the Ultimate Influence Model?
A: Show answer
Role modeling means deliberately demonstrating the values and behaviors that one hopes others will adopt. It is more specific than trying to appear like a good person: the person identifies the outcome they are trying to influence and acts in a way that makes the desired behavior observable. A manager who wants openness demonstrates openness to feedback; a leader who expects respectful disagreement listens without retaliation; a parent who wants honesty admits and corrects their own mistakes. The chapter emphasizes that people notice whether the leader embodies the standard. Credibility weakens when words and behavior contradict each other.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one behavior you expect from others and identify how you can demonstrate it visibly this week."
Key point:
- A role model makes the desired standard observable through personal conduct.
- Consistency between words and actions creates credibility.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
31. Q: How can role modeling be made specific rather than a vague aspiration to be a good person?
A: Show answer
Start with a concrete influence objective. If the desired result is better collaboration, model active listening, sharing information, acknowledging others’ contributions, and resolving disagreement directly. If the desired result is accountability, model keeping commitments, admitting errors, and communicating delays early. If the aim is innovation, model asking questions, testing assumptions, and treating useful lessons from failure seriously. The chapter distinguishes general goodness from focused behavior that helps others achieve a particular result. The leader should also explain the reasoning when appropriate, so people can understand both the behavior and the value behind it.
Study instruction:
- "Write one outcome you want to influence and list three behaviors that would demonstrate the standard to others."
Key point:
- Role modeling is most useful when connected to a specific shared outcome.
- People should see the behavior consistently in ordinary decisions, not only speeches.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
32. Q: Why did the chapter ask Juan, the CEO, to consider Daniela’s perspective?
A: Show answer
Juan initially treated Daniela’s different design vision as a threat and considered reducing her budget, moving team members, and limiting her influence. The chapter’s intervention asked him to consider what Daniela might think about his role, the team, and the company, and what challenges she might want him to accept regarding his own character, connections, and contribution. This perspective-taking exercise exposed the possibility that he was interpreting her leadership as a threat when it might be an attempt to improve the business. It also helped him see that he had stopped behaving like the collaborative leader he wanted to be. The lesson is to test assumptions and consider how one’s conduct appears from the other side.
Study instruction:
- "Map a current disagreement from both participants’ perspectives, separating known facts from unverified assumptions."
Key point:
- Perspective-taking can reveal how a leader contributes to a conflict.
- A person’s interpretation should be tested through dialogue, not treated as fact.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
33. Q: What leadership mistake did Juan make when he tried to control Daniela instead of influencing her?
A: Show answer
Juan focused on limiting Daniela’s power rather than understanding the ambition that had attracted her to the company. His proposed actions might have reduced her autonomy and intensified the split between supporters, stalled projects, and blame. He also overlooked the promise he had made to give her a platform for growth. The chapter reframes the problem: if he wanted Daniela to collaborate, he needed a shared ambition and a more effective relationship, not tactics designed to diminish her. This does not mean he had to surrender the company or accept every proposal. It means he needed to reconnect, clarify business objectives, invite dialogue, and agree on how competing ideas would be evaluated.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one control tactic used in a conflict and replace it with a conversation about shared objectives and decision criteria."
Key point:
- Control tactics can intensify resistance and organizational division.
- A shared purpose can preserve accountability while inviting collaboration.

34. Q: What did Juan change when he decided to become a better role model?
A: Show answer
Juan recognized that he needed to demonstrate the behaviors he wanted from Daniela and the wider team. He became more open to other people’s ideas, asked questions instead of simply taking fixed positions, and listened to Daniela’s perspective. He also publicly acknowledged his mistaken assumptions and invited her to explain her vision to the group. This changed the tone of the meeting from internal competition to collective problem-solving. The practical lesson is that a leader may need to change their own conduct before asking others to change theirs. The chapter does not present the outcome as magic; it links the change to specific observable actions and dialogue.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one behavior you criticize in another person and check whether you demonstrate the opposite standard yourself."
Key point:
- Credible influence starts with personal responsibility.
- Leaders should model the openness and respect they expect from others.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
35. Q: What does the story of Linda Ballew illustrate about combining praise and challenge?
A: Show answer
Linda, the author’s English teacher and journalism adviser, recognized his writing and photography while also explaining how they could improve. She did not dismiss his frustration or simply lecture him about the value of staying in school. Instead, she treated him as capable, challenged him not to define himself as a quitter, and invited him to contribute to the student newspaper. Her feedback was specific enough to show that praise and improvement could coexist. She listened to his objections, respected his perspective, and left the relationship open. Her influence came from recognizing potential, setting a meaningful challenge, and supporting action over time.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare feedback that names a genuine strength, one improvement area, and a specific opportunity to apply the learning."
Key point:
- Credible praise recognizes real strengths while honest challenge supports growth.
- Encouragement is stronger when followed by practical opportunity and support.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
36. Q: How did Linda Ballew create ownership and excellence within the student newspaper team?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Linda allowing students to make meaningful choices about headlines, photographs, layouts, and other aspects of the newspaper, even though she had more expertise. She set a high expectation for quality, helped students analyze competing publications, encouraged them to build on one another’s strengths, and remained present during demanding production periods. This combination gave students authorship while providing guidance and a shared standard. The resulting team developed competence, confidence, and camaraderie. The lesson for managers is to avoid confusing expertise with the need to control every decision: skilled coaching can create a team that is more capable than a team that merely follows instructions.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one team decision that could be delegated with clear quality criteria and a review checkpoint."
Key point:
- Ownership grows when people are trusted to make meaningful choices.
- Guidance and high standards can coexist with distributed decision-making.

37. Q: Why is asking questions part of effective role modeling?
A: Show answer
Leaders teach through the questions they ask because questions reveal what they consider important. Linda modeled curiosity by asking whether a layout was final, whether an article needed more detail, and how a decision would represent the team. A manager who asks about evidence, customer impact, quality, and learning demonstrates that careful thinking is expected. Questions also show that the leader does not assume they already possess every answer. This does not mean decisions should be endlessly delayed: the leader should establish when input is needed, who decides, and when the team will move to action. The example is disciplined inquiry followed by responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare questions that demonstrate the standards you want your team to apply before finalizing important work."
Key point:
- The questions a leader asks signal the quality of thinking expected.
- Inquiry should support timely decisions, not become avoidance.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
38. Q: How do teaching, challenge, and role modeling reinforce one another?
A: Show answer
Teaching supplies a way to interpret a situation, challenge translates that perspective into a higher standard or action, and role modeling demonstrates that the standard is real. For example, a manager may teach the team to view customer complaints as data, challenge each person to propose one service improvement, and then model openness by acting on feedback about the manager’s own process. If one part is missing, the influence can weaken: teaching without action may feel abstract, challenge without trust may feel punitive, and role modeling without explanation may leave people unsure what to learn. The three practices work best as a consistent pattern over time.
Study instruction:
- "For one current objective, define the perspective to teach, the growth challenge to issue, and the behavior to model."
Key point:
- The three practices create a reinforcing influence cycle.
- Consistency matters more than a one-off motivational conversation.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
39. Q: What does the chapter mean by the idea that the ability to influence may depend on being willing to be influenced?
A: Show answer
The closing conversation with Juan suggests that influence is reciprocal. A leader who expects others to consider new ideas must also be willing to examine their own assumptions, accept feedback, and change when the evidence warrants it. Being influenced does not mean abandoning responsibility or agreeing with every suggestion. It means listening seriously, explaining how information affects a decision, and recognizing when another person has a better idea. This openness can reduce defensiveness and make the relationship more credible. The practical test is whether the leader can identify a recent occasion when input from someone else genuinely changed their thinking or behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Record one recent example of feedback that changed your decision and one example where you explained why you did not adopt the advice."
Key point:
- Influence is stronger when it is reciprocal rather than one-directional.
- Listening does not remove decision accountability or require automatic agreement.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
40. Q: How can a person distinguish ethical influence from manipulation?
A: Show answer
Ethical influence respects another person’s autonomy, uses honest information, makes the purpose reasonably clear, and aims at a legitimate outcome that does not depend on deception. Manipulation hides material intentions, exploits vulnerabilities, distorts evidence, uses guilt or fear to bypass judgment, or treats the other person primarily as a tool. The chapter acknowledges that the same techniques could be misused, which makes intent and conduct essential. A practical safeguard is to ask whether the request would still seem fair if the recipient understood the influencer’s purpose and the relevant facts. The recipient must be able to question, disagree, or decline without retaliation.
Study instruction:
- "Review one persuasive message and check whether it withholds information, applies undue pressure, or disguises the real objective."
Key point:
- Ethical influence supports informed choice and mutual respect.
- Manipulation gains compliance by undermining another person’s judgment or agency.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
41. Q: When does a constructive challenge become coercive or harmful?
A: Show answer
A challenge becomes harmful when the person is shamed, threatened, misled, pressured beyond reasonable capacity, or punished for raising valid concerns. It can also become coercive when a leader uses their power to make refusal unsafe, sets impossible standards, or frames every disagreement as a lack of commitment. A responsible challenge is transparent about expectations, proportionate to the role, supported by resources, and open to discussion about constraints. Where formal performance, safety, or conduct rules apply, leaders should follow the relevant policies rather than substitute personal motivation techniques. The aim is to create growth and accountability without sacrificing dignity or psychological safety.
Study instruction:
- "Check a difficult challenge against four tests: clarity, proportionality, support, and freedom to raise concerns."
Key point:
- A growth challenge must preserve dignity and a fair opportunity to respond.
- Authority and organizational policy constrain how influence should be used.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
42. Q: How should a leader respond when someone resists a positive influence attempt?
A: Show answer
Resistance may indicate disagreement, mistrust, unclear expectations, lack of capacity, competing priorities, or a poor fit between the proposed challenge and the individual’s needs. The leader should ask what concern lies behind the response rather than immediately interpreting it as laziness or disloyalty. Clarify the intended outcome, invite alternative approaches, and assess whether the person has the resources and decision authority required. Some requests will still be refused or some decisions will remain contested; in that case, the leader should explain the decision and next steps fairly. Repeatedly intensifying pressure without learning from resistance is unlikely to produce sustainable commitment.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a response to resistance that includes one open question, one clarification of purpose, and one possible adjustment."
Key point:
- Resistance is information that should be understood before it is overcome.
- Respectful influence allows disagreement and may require changing the approach.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
43. Q: How can someone evaluate whether their influence is actually improving outcomes?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should combine behavior, relationship quality, and results. Relevant indicators may include whether people understand the purpose, ask more questions, share concerns earlier, take appropriate ownership, improve the quality of collaboration, meet commitments, or generate useful improvements. Feedback from the people affected is essential because a leader’s belief that they are influential may not match others’ experience. Measures should be selected for the context: a sales conversation may focus on informed customer decisions, while a team-development effort may focus on capability and coordination. Avoid treating agreement, silence, or short-term compliance as proof of positive influence. Review unintended consequences as well as intended outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two observable behavior measures and one outcome measure for an influence effort, then ask participants for feedback."
Key point:
- Influence should be evaluated by its effects, not by self-confidence alone.
- Healthy results include capability, trust, and sustainable performance.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
44. Q: How can the Ultimate Influence Model be used to plan a difficult workplace conversation?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the business or relationship outcome that must improve. For the teaching practice, identify the perspective the person may need to examine about their role, colleagues, or wider situation. For the challenge practice, define a specific next step in character, connection, or contribution, such as communicating risks earlier or collaborating across teams. For role modeling, decide what the leader must demonstrate, such as listening without interruption or acknowledging an error. Prepare evidence, choose a suitable setting, and invite the person’s view before agreeing on actions, responsibilities, and follow-up. The model structures the conversation but does not replace relevant HR, safety, or compliance procedures.
Study instruction:
- "Plan a real conversation using one teaching question, one constructive challenge, one role-model behavior, and a follow-up date."
Key point:
- The model becomes practical when translated into questions, actions, and follow-up.
- Difficult conversations must still comply with relevant policies and decision rights.
Source links:
- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
45. Q: What are the most important lessons to apply from the chapter on developing influence?
A: Show answer
Influence is a learnable, reciprocal practice built through meaningful relationships and observable conduct. Ask clearly for what is needed and ask questions that reveal the other person’s perspective. Give useful support, express genuine appreciation, and offer trust and autonomy where appropriate. Teach people to think more clearly about themselves, others, and the wider world; challenge their character, relationships, and contribution with respect; and model the values and behaviors expected of them. Avoid manipulation, humiliation, and control tactics that undermine agency. The chapter’s stories of Juan, Daniela, the author’s parents, and Linda Ballew show that durable influence begins when people feel respected, are invited to grow, and see the leader living the standard.
Study instruction:
- "Write a one-page influence plan for one relationship, including the purpose, three model practices, ethical safeguards, and measures of progress."
Key point:
- The strongest influence helps others think bigger, grow, and contribute.
- A leader earns credibility by remaining open to being influenced in return.

- Brendon Burchard: High Performance Habits
